=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
We call a positive integer n a good integer if and only if the sum of its positive divisors is divisible by 3.
You are given two positive integers N and M. Find the number, modulo 998244353, of length-M sequences A of positive integers such that the product of the elements in A is a good integer not exceeding N.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N M

Output

Print the answer.

Constraints


- 1 \leq N \leq 10^{10}
- 1 \leq M \leq 10^5
- N and M are integers.

Sample Input 1

10 1

Sample Output 1

5

There are five sequences that satisfy the conditions:

- (2)
- (5)
- (6)
- (8)
- (10)

Sample Input 2

4 2

Sample Output 2

2

There are two sequences that satisfy the conditions:

- (1, 2)
- (2, 1)

Sample Input 3

370 907

Sample Output 3

221764640

Sample Input 4

10000000000 100000

Sample Output 4

447456146

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Standard prime counting π(x) for all floor(N/i) values:

Lucy DP for sum over primes of a completely multiplicative function f(p):
Let g[n] = ∑_{2≤i≤n} f(i) (initial over all integers). Then for each prime p, if p is prime (g[p] != g[p-1]? for f maybe), update g[n] -= f(p) * (g[n//p] - g[p-1]). At the end, g[n] = ∑_{p≤n} f(p). This works for f(i) being completely multiplicative? For f(i)=1, initial g[n]=n-1, update g[n]-= (g[n//p]-g[p-1]) gives π(n). For f(i)=χ(i) (completely multiplicative mod3), initial g[n]=∑_{2≤i≤n} χ(i), update g[n]-=χ(p)*(g[n//p]-g[p-1]) gives ∑_{p≤n} χ(p). Yes.

sieve(n):
  V = [n//i]
  S = {v: v-1 for v in V}
  for p in range(2, int(sqrt(n))+1):
    if S[p] > S[p-1]:
      sp = S[p-1]
      for v in V:
        if v < p*p: break
        S[v] -= S[v//p] - sp
  return S[n]

Is this correct? Let's re-derive carefully.

Let A be a set of primes to avoid. Define F_A(Y)=∑_{a squarefree 1-smooth, a≤Y, gcd(a, rad(A))=1} M^{ω(a)}.
Pick q∈A. Let B=A\{q}.
Numbers counted by F_B(Y) either avoid q or are divisible by q.
If divisible by q, write a = q * u. Since a squarefree, u is squarefree, q∤u, and u avoids B (because a avoids B). Also u must avoid q as well for squarefree, so u avoids A. Weight M^{ω(a)} = M * M^{ω(u)}. Product bound u≤Y/q. Thus contribution = M * F_A(Y//q).
Therefore F_B(Y) = F_A(Y) + M * F_A(Y//q). So F_A(Y) = F_B(Y) - M * F_A(Y//q). Yes recurrence correct.

This recurrence reduces A size for first term, and same A with smaller Y for second. Base A empty -> G(Y). This can be memoized on (tuple(A), Y). A size≤3, Y values many. But recursion branches: for A size k, it calls size k-1 and size k with Y/q. The size k branch reduces Y by factor q≥7, so depth ≤ log_7 Y ~12. Number of states per A maybe O(log Y * 2^k). Fine.

Now, F_b(X) formula:

We expressed:

Then ∏_{p|a} w_p = M^{ω(a)} ∏_{p|a∩P} r_p.
Using identity ∏_{p∈P} [1 + (r_p-1) I_{p|a}] = ∏_{p|a∩P} r_p.
Expand:
F_b(X) = ∑_{S⊆P} (∏_{p∈S}(r_p-1)) * ∑_{a: S|a} M^{ω(a)}.
For a squarefree and S|a, write a=S*u, u squarefree, gcd(u,S)=1. Weight M^{ω(a)}=M^{|S|} M^{ω(u)}. Sum over u≤X/rad(S), avoiding S = M^{|S|} * F_S(X/rad(S)) where F_S is G_avoid(S, ·). Thus:

def calc(k, n):
    if k==0: return 1 if n>=1 else 0
    if n < 2**k? Actually if n < 2^k, T_k(n)=0 because product of k integers ≥2 ≥2^k. We can return 0.
    if k==1: return max(0, n-1) (numbers 2..n)
    memoize.
    res=0
    x=2
    while x<=n:
        v = n//x
        y = n//v
        cnt = y - x + 1
        res += cnt * calc(k-1, v)
        x = y+1
    return res

and w_p for p≡1:

For p≡2:

- if p∤c: δ=1 factor M.
For p=3 none.

- For p≡1: allowed δ=0,1,2; weights relative to base: 1, w1, w2.
- For p≡2: allowed δ=0,1; weights: 1, w1.

- For p∈P_b (p≡1, α>0): if δ=0 corr=1; δ=1 corr = r1 / M; δ=2 corr = r2 / M2.
- For p∈P_c (p≡2, β>0): if δ=0 corr=1; δ=1 corr = s1 / M.

This is a multiplicative correction over a small set of primes (≤8). We can expand over subsets and exponent choices? Since for p≡1, correction depends on whether δ=1 or2. The default weight already accounts for δ=1/2. We can express correction as:
For p∈P_b:
corr = 1 + A_p * I(δ=1) + B_p * I(δ=2), where A_p = r1/M -1, B_p = r2/M2 -1.
For p∈P_c:
corr = 1 + C_p * I(δ=1), C_p = s1/M -1.
Then the sum over a of default_weight * ∏ corr can be expanded over subsets of these indicator events. Each event corresponds to forcing a prime p to have a specific exponent (1 or2 for p≡1; 1 for p≡2). This is more complex but since number of correction primes ≤8, we can enumerate all combinations of choices for each correction prime: for each p in P_b, choose none, δ=1, δ=2; for p in P_c, choose none or δ=1. Maybe we can use the same G_avoid-style recurrence for H_all. The default weight function U(X) can be computed via small-s + prime counts. Corrections for b/c can be handled by inclusion-exclusion over small primes using functions that force exponents. Since correction primes are few, we can precompute for each possible set of correction primes and exponent choices the sum over a satisfying those forced divisibilities. This is similar to G_avoid but with exponent 1 or2.

Let's formalize.

Let default weight function:
For p≡1:
- δ=0: factor 1
- δ=1: factor A = M
- δ=2: factor B = M*(M+1)/2 mod MOD
For p≡2:
- δ=0: factor 1
- δ=1: factor A = M
No other primes.

This is a multiplicative function over A_all. Let U(X) = summatory.

For fixed b,c, correction primes:
P1 = primes dividing b (p≡1), with α.
P2 = primes dividing c (p≡2), with β.

For p∈P1:
- default factors: δ=1 -> A, δ=2 -> B.
- corrected factors: δ=1 -> r1 = g1/g0, δ=2 -> r2 = g2/g0.
So correction multiplier for δ=1: r1/A; for δ=2: r2/B.
For p∈P2:
- default δ=1 -> A, corrected -> s1 = h1/h0; multiplier s1/A.

We can expand H_{b,c}(X) as:
H = ∑_{a} default(a) * ∏_{p∈P1} (1 + u_p I1 + v_p I2) * ∏_{p∈P2} (1 + w_p I1)
where u_p = r1/A -1, v_p = r2/B -1, w_p = s1/A -1.
Expanding yields terms indexed by:
- For each p∈P1: choose none, choose δ=1, choose δ=2.
- For each p∈P2: choose none or δ=1.
Each term requires summing default(a) over a with forced exponent δ_p for chosen primes (and no restriction on others). Since a has exponents limited (1/2 for p≡1, 1 for p≡2), forcing δ=1 or2 means p divides a with that exact exponent. Because a's exponent cannot exceed2/1. For p≡1 forcing δ=2 means p^2 | a but p^3 ∤ a (automatically since exponent≤2). For p≡2 forcing δ=1 means p|a.

We can compute such restricted sums using functions similar to U but with divisibility conditions. Since correction primes are small and few, we can precompute for each possible set of forced primes/exponents? But b/c vary.

Maybe we can compute H_{b,c}(X) by direct inclusion-exclusion over the correction primes using a function U_forced(conditions, Y). Conditions are of form p^e || a (exact exponent e) for some primes. Since e is small (1 or2). We can express exact exponent conditions in terms of divisibility and avoidance:
- For p≡1, δ=2 means p^2 | a and p^3 ∤ a. But a's exponent≤2, so p^3∤a automatically? Wait a can have p≡1 exponent 2 max, so p^3 never divides a. Thus δ=2 iff p^2 | a. δ=1 iff p|a and p^2∤a. δ=0 iff p∤a.
- For p≡2, δ=1 iff p|a (since exponent max1).

Thus conditions are:
- p|a (for δ≥1)
- p^2|a (for p≡1 δ=2)
- p∤a (for δ=0, but in expansion we don't force δ=0; none choice means no condition, so a may or may not have p).

In the expansion, a term chooses for each correction prime either no condition, or p|a, or p^2|a (for P1), or p|a (for P2). We need sum over a satisfying a set of divisibility conditions (p|a or p^2|a) of default(a).

Since a is in A_all, if p^2|a then p≡1 automatically (P2 max exponent1, so p^2 condition only for P1). Divisibility conditions are independent? If we force p|a and q|a, that's fine. If force p^2|a, then p|a implied. We can factor out the forced part.

Let D be the product of forced prime powers: for each forced p with exponent e (1 or2), include p^e. Since a has exponent exactly e for those primes (because max e and forced p^e; for p≡1 e=2, exponent could be2; for p≡1 e=1, exponent could be1 or2? Wait if we force p|a but not p^2, in the expansion term for δ=1, we need exact δ=1, not just p|a. The indicator I(δ=1) is exact exponent 1. If we use condition p|a only, it includes δ=2 as well. We need exact δ=1. So for P1, choosing δ=1 requires p|a and p^2∤a. Choosing δ=2 requires p^2|a. Thus terms with δ=1 have an avoidance condition p^2∤a. This complicates.

We can express I(δ=1) = I(p|a) - I(p^2|a) for p≡1. For p≡2, I(δ=1)=I(p|a) (since max1). So in expansion, a term with δ=1 for P1 contributes to sums with p|a minus p^2|a. We can handle by expanding further: replace I1 by D_p - E_p where D_p = [p|a], E_p=[p^2|a]. Then each term is a sum over a with a set of divisibility conditions (some p|a, some p^2|a) with coefficients. This is manageable.

Similarly for P2, I1 = [p|a].

Thus H_{b,c}(X) can be expressed as a linear combination of U_cond(X; conditions), where conditions are a set of primes with required exponent 1 or2 (for p≡1) and 1 (for p≡2). Coefficients are products of correction differences.

Number of conditions sets: For each P1 prime, possible contribution from factor (1 + u(D-E) + v E) = 1 + u D + (v-u) E. So choices: none, D, E. For P2: 1 + w D. So total combinations 3^{|P1|} * 2^{|P2|} ≤ 864. Still.

F_corrected(Y) = F(Y) + (corr1 -1) * F_{δ=1}(Y) + (corr2 -1) * F_{δ=2}(Y) for p≡1.
Where F_{δ=1} = sum over a with exact exponent1. This can be expressed via F and F_{p^2|a} etc. We can compute recursively. Maybe T_k(N) for k≤33 can be computed using a simple recursion with memoization and pruning that is actually fast enough. Let's test more concretely with pruning and precomputed small values.

We can compute T_k(n) recursively:
def T(k,n):
    if k==0: return 1
    if n < (1<<k): return 0
    if k==1: return n-1
    if k==2: return sum_{x=2}^{n} (n//x -1)  # can compute O(√n)
    # maybe for k==3 direct formula?
    memo
    res=0
    x=2
    limit = n // (1<<(k-1))  # because v>=2^{k-1}
    while x <= limit:
        v = n//x
        y = min(limit, n//v)
        cnt = y - x + 1
        res += cnt * T(k-1, v)
        x = y+1
    return res
This loops over x up to limit, not all n. For k=3, limit = n//4. Number of distinct quotients for x≤n//4. For n=N, limit=2.5e9 >√n, so distinct quotients ~2√n=2e5. For k=3, states n∈V with n≥8. Many states. But T(2,v) can be computed O(√v) if not memo? We can precompute T(2,v) for all v in V? T_2(v)=∑_{x=2}^v (v//x -1). This can be computed O(√v) per v, too much. But T_2(v) has a known formula using divisor summatory: T_2(v) = ∑_{x=2}^v (⌊v/x⌋ -1) = D_2(v) - 2v +1? Let's derive:
D_2(v)=∑_{x=1}^v floor(v/x). T_2(v)=∑_{x=2}^v (floor(v/x)-1) = D_2(v) - v - (v-1) = D_2(v) - 2v +1. D_2(v) can be computed O(√v). Still per state.

Maybe we can compute H_{b,c}(X) using the same G_avoid-style base functions and corrections, but with corrections over small primes. Since P_b/P_c small, we can precompute base U(X) and functions for forced divisibility, and compute corrections via inclusion-exclusion in O(2^{|P|}) per query, not O(S_all). This is the way.

Let's develop the correction expansion for H_all.

We need base functions:
- U(Y) = ∑_{a∈A_all, a≤Y} default_weight(a).
- For conditions: for a set of primes with required exponent 1 or2 (only p≡1 can require2), we need U_cond(Y) = ∑_{a∈A_all, a≤Y, a satisfies conditions} default_weight(a).

Conditions can be represented as a divisor d (product of p^e for forced primes) and a set of primes that must not divide a? Wait exact exponent conditions:
- For p≡1, δ=1 exact means p|a and p^2∤a. In inclusion-exclusion, we can express using U_{p|a} - U_{p^2|a}.
- For p≡2, δ=1 exact means p|a (since max1).
So any term in expansion is a linear combination of sums with conditions of the form: for some primes, require p|a; for some primes, require p^2|a. There are no avoidance conditions except those handled by subtraction. Thus we need functions F_C(Y) where C is a set of pairs (p, e) with e=1 or2, requiring p^e | a. Since a's exponents are limited, requiring p^e|a is equivalent to a = p^e * u, with u∈A_all, and if e=2 for p≡1, u cannot contain p (because max exponent2). If e=1 for p≡1, u may contain p? Wait if we require p|a but not p^2, that's not a simple divisibility condition. But in the inclusion-exclusion, we use U_{p|a} - U_{p^2|a}. U_{p|a} allows u to contain p (so a exponent 2). U_{p^2|a} forces exponent2. The difference is exact exponent1. So we need functions for divisibility by p and by p^2, not exact. Good.

For a set of divisibility conditions (some p^1, some p^2), since a is in A_all, if we require p^2 for a p≡1, then a has p exponent exactly2 (because max2). If we require p^1 for p≡1, a may have exponent1 or2. If we require p^1 for p≡2, a has exponent exactly1 (max1). If we require both p^1 and p^2 for same p, p^2 implies p^1; we can simplify.

Thus F_C(Y) = ∑_{a∈A_all, a≤Y, d|a} default_weight(a), where d = ∏ p^{e_p} for conditions. Since a's exponents limited, d must be valid (e_p≤2 for p≡1, e_p≤1 for p≡2). Then a = d * u, with u∈A_all and gcd? If d has p^2 for p≡1, u cannot contain p (otherwise exponent>2). If d has p^1 for p≡1, u may contain p (exponent2) or not. If d has p^1 for p≡2, u cannot contain p (max1). So u must avoid primes where d has reached the maximum exponent (p≡1 e=2, p≡2 e=1), and for p≡1 e=1, u may or may not contain p. This is messy but manageable with avoidance sets.

However, the number of conditions in a term is at most |P1|+|P2|≤8. We can compute F_C(Y) by factoring out d and summing over u with restrictions. This is similar to G_avoid but with default weights and partial restrictions. Could be complex.

Let's reconsider T_k computation. Maybe it's simpler than H_all correction. We can implement T_k with memoization and pruning, and it might pass within time if optimized. Let's estimate more realistically with pruning and precomputed T_2.

We can compute T_k(N) for k=0..L using a recursive function with memoization and early cutoff. Let's implement a small script mentally? We can't run. But we can reason.

The recurrence with pruning:
T(k,n):
 if k==0: return 1
 if n < (1<<k): return 0
 if k==1: return n-1
 if k==2: return D2(n) - 2*n + 1
 memo
 res=0
 x=2
 limit = n // (1<<(k-1))
 while x <= limit:
    v = n//x
    y = min(limit, n//v)
    res += (y-x+1) * T(k-1, v)
    x = y+1
 return res

For k=3, limit=n//4. For n=N, loop over distinct quotients for x≤2.5e9. Since limit>√n, number of distinct quotients ~2√n=2e5. It calls T(2,v) for each quotient v. T(2,v) computed via D2(v) O(√v) if not memoized. But T(2,v) for v values are many; computing D2(v) for each v would be ∑√v over v in V ~84M, maybe okay? But T(3,N) only calls T(2,v) for v = floor(N/x) for x≤N/4. That's about 2e5 v values. Computing D2 for each v O(√v) sum over v of √v? v ranges from N/4 down to4. Sum over distinct quotients of floor(N/x) of √v. This is similar to sum over x of sqrt(N/x) for x≤√N? Let's approximate: v values are N//x. For x≤√N, v≥√N, count √N values, sum sqrt(v) ≈√N * √N = N? Actually ∑_{x=1}^{√N} sqrt(N/x) = √N * 2 N^{1/4} =2 N^{3/4}=2*1e7.5=6.3e7? Wait N=1e10, N^{3/4}=1e7.5=31.6M, times2=63M. For x>√N, v<√N, but limit=N/4 >√N, so x up to N/4 includes many x where v small; grouping reduces. The sum of sqrt(v) over distinct v maybe ∑_{v=1}^{√N} sqrt(v) ≈2/3 N^{3/4}=21M plus large part 63M =84M. So T(3,N) would do 84M operations just for T2 calls, too high.

But we can memoize T(2,v) for v in V; computing D2(v) for all v in V once costs 84M, maybe high but perhaps acceptable in Py? 84M is too high.

Maybe we can compute D2(v) for all v in V faster. D2(v)=∑_{i=1}^v floor(v/i). For v in V, we can compute using the same grouping per v; sum over v in V of √v ~84M. Not good.

Could precompute D2(n) for n up to B=1e5 via sieve, and for n>B compute O(√n). Large v in V are N/i for i≤1e5. For each such v, √v ~1e5/√i. Sum over i=1..1e5 of 1e5/√i =1e5*632=63M. Still high.

Now, we need generate S_all and S_small lists with factors and default weights.

Let's define B = isqrt(N) = 100000.

Primes:
- primes1 = primes ≡1 mod3 up to B.
- primes2 = primes ≡2 mod3 up to B.

S_small (bad a-set): squarefree numbers composed only of primes1, ≤B. For each s, store:
  value s
  default_weight = M^{ω(s)} mod MOD (for bad base)
  factors = list of primes1 dividing s (for corrections with b)
We can generate by DFS over primes1, squarefree. Since max depth small.

S_all (total a-set): numbers ≤B composed of primes1 with exponent 0..2 and primes2 with exponent 0..1. For each s, store:
  value s
  default_weight = ∏ default factors:
    for p≡1: if exponent 1 -> M; if exponent2 -> M2 = M*(M+1)/2 mod MOD.
    for p≡2: if exponent1 -> M.
  factors1 = list of (p, exp) for p≡1 (exp 1 or2)
  factors2 = list of p for p≡2 (exp1)
Need for corrections:
  For p≡1 in b with α>0:
    correction for exp δ=1: corr1 = r1 / M, where r1 = g1/g0.
    correction for exp δ=2: corr2 = r2 / M2, where r2 = g2/g0.
  For p≡2 in c with β>0:
    correction for exp δ=1: corr2p = s1 / M, where s1 = h1/h0.
If p not in b/c, correction=1.
So w_s(b,c) = default_weight(s) * ∏_{p,exp in factors1, p∈P_b} corr1_p if exp==1 else corr2_p * ∏_{p in factors2, p∈P_c} corr2p_p.

We can precompute for each b a dict corr1_b[p] = (corr for δ=1, corr for δ=2) for p in P_b. For each c, dict corr_c[p] = corr for δ=1.

Then computing w_s for a given b,c by iterating factors of s:
- For s, factors1 list small (≤4), factors2 list small. For each factor, check if p in b_dict/c_dict. We can do dict lookups.

To speed, we can store for each s a tuple of primes in factors1 and exponents, and primes in factors2. Since s≤1e5, number of factors small.

Now, outer sum for total and bad:
We need enumerate b_list and c_list.

b_list: all 1-smooth numbers ≤CUBE_LIMIT (floor cube root N). For each b:
- value b
- base_b = ∏_{p|b} g0(α) = C(3α+M-1,M-1)
- corr1_b dict: for p, α:
    g0 = C(3α+M-1, M-1)
    g1 = C(3α+M, M-1)
    g2 = C(3α+M+1, M-1)  # since e=3α+2 -> C(3α+M+1, M-1)
    r1 = g1 * inv(g0) % MOD
    r2 = g2 * inv(g0) % MOD
    corr1 = r1 * invM % MOD
    corr2 = r2 * invM2 % MOD  # M2 = M*(M+1)/2
    store.
- For bad F_b, we need w_p(b) = r1 for δ=1 (since a squarefree, only δ=1). In bad S_small, correction for p|s is r1 / M = corr1. So we can use same corr1.
- For bad total_small_weight_bad[b], we can compute using S_small default M^{ω} * ∏ corr1 for p|s∩P_b.

c_list: all 2-smooth numbers ≤B (since c≤sqrtN). For each c:
- value c
- weight_c = ∏_{p|c} h0(β) = C(2β+M-1,M-1)
- corr_c dict: for p, β:
    h0 = C(2β+M-1, M-1)
    h1 = C(2β+M, M-1)  # e=2β+1 -> C(2β+M, M-1)
    s1 = h1 * inv(h0) % MOD
    corr = s1 * invM % MOD
    store.

t: max_t = floor(log_3 N). Precompute comb_t[t] = C(t+M-1, M-1).

Now, outer loops:
For each b in b_list:
  b3 = b^3
  c_limit = isqrt(N // b3)  # because c^2 ≤ N/b3
  # Precompute bad_total_small[b] if not done
  # For total, we may need total_weight_all for (b,c) when X>=B; compute on demand.
  For each c in c_list with c <= c_limit:
    D_bc = b3 * c * c
    # t loop
    pow3 = 1
    for t in range(max_t+1):
       D = D_bc * pow3
       if D > N: break
       X = N // D
       if X == 0: break
       C_t = comb_t[t]
       # Bad contribution:
       F_bad = compute_F_bad(b, X)
       S_bad += base_b * C_t * c.weight_c * F_bad
       # Total contribution:
       H_all = compute_H_all(b, c, X)
       S_total += base_b * c.weight_c * C_t * H_all
       pow3 *= 3

But note for bad, the outer sum in previous formula was S_bad = ∑ Base_b * C_t * C_c * F_b(X), where F_b(X) sums over a squarefree 1-smooth with weights w_p(b). This is exactly compute_F_bad(b,X). Good.

For total, S_total = ∑ Base_b * C_c * C_t * H_{b,c}(X), where Base_b includes p≡1 g0, C_c includes p≡2 h0, and H includes a weights relative to base. Good.

Then answer = (S_total - S_bad) mod MOD. Because good = total - bad.

We need ensure total_all includes all sequences product≤N. The decomposition n = a b^3 c^2 3^t is unique for all n. The weight d_M(n) factorizes as Base_b * C_c * C_t * (a relative weights). Yes.

  if limit > 0:
     for s in S_small until s>limit:

           first += w_s(b) * M * (pi1[q] - pi1[B])? Wait formula: H = first + M * ∑ w_s * (π1(q)-π1(B)). So second = M * sum...
     Actually first already includes s≤X. For s≤limit, q>B, so add M * w_s * (π1(q)-π1(B)).
  Return first mod MOD.
But if X < B, limit = X//(B+1)=0, second none. first iterates S_small≤X.

Pseudo:
def gen_all(idx, cur, weight, factors1, factors2):
    add (cur, weight, tuple(factors1), tuple(factors2))
    for i in range(idx, len(primes_all)):
        p=primes_all[i]
        maxe = 2 if p%3==1 else 1
        val = cur
        for e in range(1, maxe+1):
            val *= p
            if val > B: break
            new_weight = weight
            if p%3==1:
                if e==1: new_weight = weight * M % MOD
                else: new_weight = weight * M2 % MOD
                new_f1 = factors1 + [(p,e)]
                new_f2 = factors2
            else:
                new_weight = weight * M % MOD
                new_f1 = factors1
                new_f2 = factors2 + [p]
            gen_all(i+1, val, new_weight, new_f1, new_f2)
But this will generate numbers where a prime can be skipped and later included. Since we process increasing, unique. However, if we add current at each node, we include 1. Good.

Now, compute bad_total_weight[b]:
def bad_total(b):
   total=0
   for s in S_small:
      w = s.default_weight
      for p in s.factors1:
         for (bp,c1,c2) in b.corr_tuple:
            if bp==p:
               w = w * c1 % MOD
               break
      total = (total + w) % MOD
   return total

Sieve primes up to B:
def sieve(n):
    is_prime = [True]*(n+1)
    is_prime[0]=is_prime[1]=False
    for i in range(2,int(n**0.5)+1):
       if is_prime[i]:
          step=i; start=i*i
          is_prime[start:n+1:step] = [False]*(((n-start)//step)+1)
    return [i for i in range(2,n+1) if is_prime[i]]
For n=1e5, fine.

Generate vals:
vals=[]
i=1
while i<=N:
    v=N//i
    vals.append(v)
    i=N//v+1
vals descending. m=len(vals). idx={v:i for i,v in enumerate(vals)}.

S0 = [v-1 for v in vals]
S1 = [0 if v%3==1 else -1 for v in vals]

For p in primes:
    if p*p > N: break (but B=sqrtN, all)
    sp0 = S0[idx[p-1]]
    sp1 = S1[idx[p-1]]
    chi = 1 if p%3==1 else -1 if p%3==2 else 0
    for pos, v in enumerate(vals):
        if v < p*p: break
        j = idx[v//p]
        S0[pos] -= S0[j] - sp0
        if chi:
            S1[pos] -= chi * (S1[j] - sp1)

After loop:

compute weight_s_all(s,b,c):
s = (value, default_weight, factors1, factors2)
w = s[1]
for p, exp in s[2]:
    for (bp,c1,c2) in b[2]:
        if bp==p:
            w = w * (c1 if exp==1 else c2) % MOD
            break
for p in s[3]:
    for (cp,corr) in c[2]:
        if cp==p:
            w = w * corr % MOD
            break
return w

def dfs(n, idx):
    ans = G(n) - G(primes[idx-1])
    for i in range(idx, len(primes)):
        p = primes[i]
        if p*p > n: break
        pe = p

            pe *= p
            if pe > n: break
            ans += f(p^e) * dfs(n//pe, i+1)
            if pe*p <= n:

    return ans

```
ll S(n, y) {
    if (p[y] > n) return 0;
    ll ans = g(n) - g(p[y-1]);
    for (int i = y; i <= cnt && p[i]*p[i] <= n; i++) {
        ll pe = p[i];
        for (int e = 1; pe * p[i] <= n; e++, pe *= p[i]) {
            ans += f(p[i], e) * S(n / pe, i+1);
            ans += f(p[i], e+1);
        }
    }
    return ans;
}
```

bad_sum = (1 + solve(N, 0, 1)) % MOD
ans = (total_all - bad_sum) % MOD

Need ensure solve excludes 1. Yes.

Let's test sample 1 N=10 M=1:
total_all should 10. solve total should 9.
bad_sum should 5. solve bad should 4 (bad n>1: 1? bad numbers ≤10: 1,3,4,7,9? Let's list good sample: 2,5,6,8,10 good. Bad:1,3,4,7,9 =5. solve bad=4). Min_25 should.

Now, let's think about f_bad for p=3. Bad allows p=3 any exponent. f_bad(3^e)=C(e+M-1,M-1). For M=1, f=1. p=3 prime sum includes 3. Good.

Now, implement Lucy DP for pi and pi1. We already have.

Let's write final code with Min_25.

Potential performance of solve with lru_cache and recursion:
The loop inside solve iterates primes from idx to √n. For top-level n=N, idx=0, iterates all primes up to1e5 (9592). For each prime, while pe*p≤n. For p=2, e up to33; for large p, e=1. So top-level operations ~9592 + extra. Each recursive call solve(n//pe, i+1) will again loop over primes from i+1 to √(n//pe). This is standard. Total operations maybe a few hundred thousand, not millions. Should be fine.

But lru_cache with idx as argument: The loop uses `for i in range(idx, len(primes))` and calls solve(n//pe, i+1). If many states, the loop ranges can be large. However, standard Min_25 without memoization is O(N^{3/4}/log). With memoization, maybe less. We'll trust.

Need be careful with recursion depth: Depth at most number of distinct prime factors ≤5? Actually product of smallest primes quickly exceeds N, so depth <10. Fine.

Now, let's write code.

One subtlety: In solve, prime_sum(n, typ) - prime_sum(prev, typ) where prev = primes[idx-1] if idx>0 else 0. But prime_sum expects v in V. primes[idx-1] is ≤1e5, in V. 0 not in V; handle prime_sum(0)=0.

Also, if idx >= len(primes) or primes[idx] > n, return 0. But what if idx < len(primes) and primes[idx] <= n but n < primes[idx]^2? The loop breaks immediately, ans = prime_sum(n)-prime_sum(prev) counts primes from idx to n. This is correct for numbers that are single primes. For n small, solve returns prime count.

Now, f_val for bad p=3: p%3==0. We check if p==3. For p=3, e any allowed. For p≡1, allowed e%3 !=2. For p≡2, allowed e%2==0. Good.

Let's implement factorial max_n = M + 100000. But if M=1e5, max_n=200000. If M small, still 100000+? Actually M+100000. For N=1e10, e max33, so M+100000 overkill. But okay.

Wait, if M=1e5, max_n=200000, fact list 200001. If M=1, max_n=100001. Good.

Now, Lucy DP for pi1: We need primes list for sieve up to B. B=isqrt(N). For N=1e10, B=100000. For N small, B small. The Min_25 primes list should be primes up to B. Good.

Let's code Lucy DP carefully with vals descending. The `idx` dict maps v to position. In inner loop, `j = idx[v // p]`. Since v//p is in vals. For p large, v//p might be < p? Still in vals. Good.

After Lucy DP, pi and pi1 dicts. For prime_sum, we need pi[v] for v in V. For v maybe 0? We handle.

Now, solve uses `primes` list from sieve. But Lucy DP also used primes. We'll have primes list.

Let's write final code.

Potential issue: In Lucy DP, for S1 initial, if v%3==1 ->0 else -1. For v=0? vals no 0. Good.

solve(10,0,0):

  ans += f(2,1)=1 * solve(5,1,0) + f(2,2)=1

Now, for bad M=1, prime_sum_bad(v)=π1(v)+(v>=3). For v=10, π1=1 (7), +1=2 (primes 3,7). solve bad should count bad n>1. Let's trust.

Now, one more thing: Min_25 computes summatory of f(n) over n with prime factors ≥primes[idx]. It assumes f is multiplicative and f(1)=1. Our f_bad for p≡2 e odd is0, but f_bad(p^2) nonzero. The recursion handles.

Let's ensure f_val for bad p=3: p==3, return val. For p=3, e any. Good.

Now, final answer modulo. Since solve returns mod, total_all = (1 + solve(...)) % MOD.

Need import sys, math, functools.

Potential recursion limit: set sys.setrecursionlimit(1000000).

Let's produce final code.

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys
import math
from functools import lru_cache

MOD = 998244353

def main():
    N, M = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split())
    M_mod = M % MOD

    # ---------- sieve ----------
    B = math.isqrt(N)
    is_prime = [True] * (B + 1)
    if B >= 0:
        is_prime[0] = False
    if B >= 1:
        is_prime[1] = False
    for i in range(2, math.isqrt(B) + 1):
        if is_prime[i]:
            step = i
            start = i * i
            is_prime[start:B + 1:step] = [False] * (((B - start) // step) + 1)
    primes = [i for i in range(2, B + 1) if is_prime[i]]

    # ---------- factorials for C(e+M-1, M-1) ----------
    max_n = M + 100000  # enough for all exponents appearing in N <= 1e10
    fact = [1] * (max_n + 1)
    for i in range(1, max_n + 1):
        fact[i] = fact[i - 1] * i % MOD
    invfact = [1] * (max_n + 1)
    invfact[max_n] = pow(fact[max_n], MOD - 2, MOD)
    for i in range(max_n, 0, -1):
        invfact[i - 1] = invfact[i] * i % MOD

    def comb(n, k):
        if k < 0 or k > n:
            return 0
        return fact[n] * invfact[k] % MOD * invfact[n - k] % MOD

    def local_factor(p, e):
        return comb(e + M - 1, M - 1)

    # ---------- Lucy DP: pi(x) and pi_1(x) for all x = floor(N/i) ----------
    vals = []
    i = 1
    while i <= N:
        v = N // i
        vals.append(v)
        i = N // v + 1
    # vals is descending

    idx = {v: i for i, v in enumerate(vals)}
    m = len(vals)

    # S0: sum over primes of 1 -> pi
    # S1: sum over primes of chi(p), chi = 1 if p==1 mod3, -1 if p==2 mod3, 0 if p==3
    S0 = [v - 1 for v in vals]
    S1 = [0 if v % 3 == 1 else -1 for v in vals]

    for p in primes:
        if p * p > N:
            break
        sp0 = S0[idx[p - 1]]
        sp1 = S1[idx[p - 1]]
        chi = 1 if p % 3 == 1 else (-1 if p % 3 == 2 else 0)
        for pos, v in enumerate(vals):
            if v < p * p:
                break
            j = idx[v // p]
            S0[pos] -= S0[j] - sp0
            if chi:
                S1[pos] -= chi * (S1[j] - sp1)

    pi = {v: S0[i] for i, v in enumerate(vals)}
    pi_chi = {v: S1[i] for i, v in enumerate(vals)}
    pi1 = {v: (pi[v] + pi_chi[v]) // 2 for v in vals}

    def prime_sum_total(x):
        if x <= 1:
            return 0
        return M_mod * pi[x] % MOD

    def prime_sum_bad(x):
        if x <= 1:
            return 0
        # bad prime: p == 1 mod 3, or p == 3
        cnt = pi1[x] + (1 if x >= 3 else 0)
        return M_mod * cnt % MOD

    def f_value(p, e, typ):
        val = local_factor(p, e)
        if typ == 0:
            return val
        # bad
        if p == 3:
            return val
        r = p % 3
        if r == 1:
            return val if e % 3 != 2 else 0
        else:  # r == 2
            return val if e % 2 == 0 else 0

    @lru_cache(maxsize=None)
    def solve(n, start_idx, typ):
        if start_idx >= len(primes):
            return 0
        if primes[start_idx] > n:
            return 0

        if typ == 0:
            ps = prime_sum_total
        else:
            ps = prime_sum_bad

        prev = primes[start_idx - 1] if start_idx > 0 else 0
        ans = ps(n) - (ps(prev) if prev else 0)
        ans %= MOD

        for i in range(start_idx, len(primes)):
            p = primes[i]
            if p * p > n:
                break
            pe = p
            e = 1
            while pe * p <= n:
                fpe = f_value(p, e, typ)
                if fpe:
                    ans += fpe * solve(n // pe, i + 1, typ)
                    ans %= MOD
                ans += f_value(p, e + 1, typ)
                ans %= MOD
                pe *= p
                e += 1

        return ans % MOD

    total_all = (1 + solve(N, 0, 0)) % MOD
    bad_sum = (1 + solve(N, 0, 1)) % MOD
    ans = (total_all - bad_sum) % MOD
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
